Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.

It’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.

It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.9, F06.7, F09, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my mind just feels foggy lately. I work around cataloguing notes at the library, old ones I’ve used for years, and I can’t even keep my place in them sometimes. I’ll be looking at a page and then—well, it reminds me of how we used to file everything by hand before the computer system changed, which was a whole mess—but now I just sort of lose the thread. Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.9, F06.7, F09, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my mind just feels foggy lately. I work around cataloguing notes at the library, old ones I’ve used for years, and I can’t even keep my place in them sometimes. I’ll be looking at a page and then—well, it reminds me of how we used to file everything by hand before the computer system changed, which was a whole mess—but now I just sort of lose the thread. Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? Little things you meant to get done, people you meant to call back.

I do think sometimes about not being as useful as I used to be. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.

But no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.

Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind feels foggy, and I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes or decide what simple thing to do next.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. My mind feels foggy, like I can’t quite get hold of the next thought.

I used to have these old cataloguing notes — little systems and abbreviations, things that would make sense to me right away — and now I lose my place in them. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, what was I looking for?” It’s embarrassing, really.

And it’s not only reading. Even deciding what simple thing to do next, like whether to sort one pile first or another, feels harder than it should. I sort of sit there, stuck, and then I’ve gone off thinking about something else entirely.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my mind just feels foggy lately. I work around cataloguing notes at the library, old ones I’ve used for years, and I can’t even keep my place in them sometimes. I’ll be looking at a page and then—well, it reminds me of how we used to file everything by hand before the computer system changed, which was a whole mess—but now I just sort of lose the thread. Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? Little things you meant to get done, people you meant to call back.\n\nI do think sometimes about not being as useful as I used to be. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. My mind feels foggy, like I can’t quite get hold of the next thought.\n\nI used to have these old cataloguing notes — little systems and abbreviations, things that would make sense to me right away — and now I lose my place in them. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, what was I looking for?” It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd it’s not only reading. Even deciding what simple thing to do next, like whether to sort one pile first or another, feels harder than it should. I sort of sit there, stuck, and then I’ve gone off thinking about something else entirely.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 17\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"depressive_episode_duration","name":"Depressive Episode Duration","description":"3 months","reason":"The doctor is asking about the duration and near-daily frequency of the depressed mood, which this item directly covers."}

Yes… yes, I think so. More than two weeks, certainly. It’s been most days for, oh, about three months now, if I’m placing it right.

It seemed to start around when I was getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through — drawers, labels, old forms no one uses anymore — and I remember thinking I should feel something clear about it, but mostly I just felt low and foggy. And that’s sort of stayed with me most days since.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.9, F06.7, F09, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my mind just feels foggy lately. I work around cataloguing notes at the library, old ones I’ve used for years, and I can’t even keep my place in them sometimes. I’ll be looking at a page and then—well, it reminds me of how we used to file everything by hand before the computer system changed, which was a whole mess—but now I just sort of lose the thread. Even deciding what simple thing to do next feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I haven’t really sat and thought it through like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? Little things you meant to get done, people you meant to call back.\n\nI do think sometimes about not being as useful as I used to be. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. My mind feels foggy, like I can’t quite get hold of the next thought.\n\nI used to have these old cataloguing notes — little systems and abbreviations, things that would make sense to me right away — and now I lose my place in them. I sort of sit there, stuck, and then I’ve gone off thinking about something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. More than two weeks, certainly. It’s been most days for, oh, about three months now, if I’m placing it right.\n\nIt seemed to start around when I was getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through — drawers, labels, old forms no one uses anymore — and I remember thinking I should feel something clear about it, but mostly I just felt low and foggy. And that’s sort of stayed with me most days since.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything. I used to like looking through my old watercolor books — I’ve had some of them since before the library switched catalog systems, actually — but now I open them and they just make me feel heavy, not interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, some mornings I do wonder what the point is of getting up and doing the same little routines — make coffee, check the mail, that kind of thing, though half the mail is just flyers now, not like when bills came proper and you had to sort them — but… life not worth living? I don’t know. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anybody, I suppose… winter months used to get long, especially when the library got quiet after the holidays and there’d be all those returns piling up in odd places. But whether it was like this, like really the same kind of depressed… I don’t know. It’s hard for me to sort that out.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to think of a thing that set it off, but nothing obvious comes to mind. Sometimes stress sneaks up on you, I suppose — like at work, when one little stack of forms becomes three stacks and then someone changes the way the labels are done — but I can’t point to one particular thing and say, yes, that’s when this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, maybe not so much conversations, I don’t always notice until afterward, but I came in because my head feels foggy. Like I can’t keep my place in my old cataloguing notes anymore, and those used to be second nature to me. I’ll be looking at a simple task and just… not know what to do next. It’s embarrassing, really. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? Little things you meant to get done, people you meant to call back.\n\nI do think sometimes about not being as useful as I used to be. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. My mind feels foggy, like I can’t quite get hold of the next thought.\n\nI used to have these old cataloguing notes — little systems and abbreviations, things that would make sense to me right away — and now I lose my place in them. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, what was I looking for?” It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd it’s not only reading. Even deciding what simple thing to do next, like whether to sort one pile first or another, feels harder than it should. I sort of sit there, stuck, and then I’ve gone off thinking about something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. More than two weeks, certainly. It’s been most days for, oh, about three months now, if I’m placing it right.\n\nIt seemed to start around when I was getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through — drawers, labels, old forms no one uses anymore — and I remember thinking I should feel something clear about it, but mostly I just felt low and foggy. And that’s sort of stayed with me most days since.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after you left the library?\n\nPatient: Around that time, yes. Not exactly after-after, if that makes sense. It was when I was getting ready to leave the library — packing up little things, old labels, stacks of forms nobody had touched in years.\n\nI kept thinking I’d misplace something important, though half of it was probably rubbish. The low feeling and the fogginess sort of settled in then, and it’s been there most days since — about three months now, I’d say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, less, I suppose. I don’t always notice it at first, but I have to remind myself to eat.

My wife notices more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve left half your rice,” or the soup will be sitting there gone cold. I never used to do that — I was brought up not to waste a meal. The notes have all these little abbreviations I made years ago, back when we still kept more paper records, and now I stare at them like someone else wrote them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your finances, like paying bills correctly?\n\nPatient: Yes… or, well, not always bills exactly, but the paperwork at home has gotten away from me. I used to keep little piles sorted — household things, travel notes, even old lecture plans from programs at the library — and now it’s all just sitting there. I’ll mean to organize it and then I can’t seem to figure out where to start, so it piles up. It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks like managing your medications or getting to places on your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need someone taking me places, no… not usually. But everyday tasks, yes, in a way. It’s more that I can’t organize myself like I used to. The lecture plans and travel notes and even simple household papers just keep piling up, and I look at them and think, well, I’ll sort that after tea, or after I find the right folder… and then it’s another week. I used to be very good with that sort of thing, you know, all the little categories. Now I don’t seem to get started.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you were or what day it was?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve been standing somewhere and not knowing where I am, if that’s what you mean. Days can blur a bit, but then, since I’m not on the same work rhythm as before, Tuesdays and Thursdays feel oddly alike. At the library we used to have the children’s program on certain days, and that sort of fixed the week in place… now I sometimes have to look at the calendar, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been lost exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you truly did not know what day or month it was?\n\nPatient: Truly not know? I don’t think so… I mean, I may forget whether it’s Wednesday or Thursday until I look at the calendar, but the month—no, I don’t think I’ve been that mixed up. Though sometimes around the holidays it all runs together, doesn’t it? The library used to have displays for every season, so you always knew where you were in the year. Now without that, I just have to check more often.\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and organization problems start before you began feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it neatly, to be honest. What made me come in was that my mind feels foggy, and I can’t keep my place even in my old cataloguing notes — things I used to follow without thinking. I’ll look at a simple task and not be able to decide what to do next.\n\nIt’s funny, because cataloguing has its own order, you know, everything has a place, and I used to find that calming. Now I can sit there with the notes in front of me and just sort of lose the thread. So whether that started before the low mood or along with it… I don’t know. It’s all tangled together for me.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and organization problems made you unable to handle your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: The bills… I haven’t, I mean, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped managing them, but the paperwork has definitely piled up. It’s not just bills, really — I’ve got household forms sitting there, travel notes I meant to sort out, even old lecture plans I was trying to organize, and I just can’t seem to put them in order the way I used to.\n\nI used to be pretty methodical with that kind of thing. At the library, if something came in with the wrong slip or the wrong code, I could sort it out. Now I look at a stack of papers on the table and it’s like… I don’t know where to begin. So yes, it’s made handling that stuff on my own harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down, like it takes much more effort to think or get yourself moving?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s right. My body feels slow, like even getting myself to the corner store takes more effort than it should. I’ll stand there thinking, “It’s only a short walk,” but it feels like I have to talk myself through it.\n\nAnd sorting papers on my desk — that used to be nothing, really. At the library we had carts and stacks and slips everywhere, and I could just move through it. Now I sit down and it’s like my hands and my head are both moving through syrup. It takes a lot more effort than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I go to bed tired — properly tired — but then I wake up around four in the morning, and that’s it. I just lie there.\n\nIt’s that quiet part of the morning, you know, when nothing’s open and there’s no reason to get up, but I’m awake anyway, thinking about what I’m supposed to do with myself now. I’ll try to stay still and not make a whole business of it, but my mind just sort of starts turning things over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worthless, exactly. Guilty… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something, don’t they? Little things you meant to get done, people you meant to call back.\n\nI do think sometimes about not being as useful as I used to be. At the library, people knew they could ask me where something was filed or what form went where, and I liked that. There was a man who always returned his books late — always had some excuse about the bus schedule — and I’d still know his card number before he said it. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut no, I don’t know if it’s excessive guilt. I wouldn’t want to say that if it’s not the right word. It’s more… I notice I’m not keeping up, and that doesn’t feel very good.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating, such as when reading or following a television program?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually… that’s part of why I came in. My mind feels foggy, like I can’t quite get hold of the next thought.\n\nI used to have these old cataloguing notes — little systems and abbreviations, things that would make sense to me right away — and now I lose my place in them. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, what was I looking for?” It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd it’s not only reading. Even deciding what simple thing to do next, like whether to sort one pile first or another, feels harder than it should. I sort of sit there, stuck, and then I’ve gone off thinking about something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. More than two weeks, certainly. It’s been most days for, oh, about three months now, if I’m placing it right.\n\nIt seemed to start around when I was getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through — drawers, labels, old forms no one uses anymore — and I remember thinking I should feel something clear about it, but mostly I just felt low and foggy. And that’s sort of stayed with me most days since.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after you left the library?\n\nPatient: Around that time, yes. Not exactly after-after, if that makes sense. It was when I was getting ready to leave the library — packing up little things, old labels, stacks of forms nobody had touched in years.\n\nI kept thinking I’d misplace something important, though half of it was probably rubbish. The low feeling and the fogginess sort of settled in then, and it’s been there most days since — about three months now, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Since leaving the library, have you found yourself constantly preoccupied with that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no. I mean, I do think about it, because the library was… well, it was where I knew where things belonged, you know? The stamps, the slips, the way certain shelves had their own little logic.\n\nBut “preoccupied” sounds like I’m sitting there all day turning it over and over, and I don’t know that that’s quite right. Sometimes something reminds me of it — like I’ll see an old due-date card or even a certain kind of pencil, funny enough — and then my mind goes off for a bit. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, less, I suppose. I don’t always notice it at first, but I have to remind myself to eat.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve left half your rice,” or the soup will be sitting there gone cold. I never used to do that — I was brought up not to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually, especially with rice, you didn’t leave grains in the bowl… But yes, lately I just don’t seem to finish things the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
